This ratio is derived from the stoichiometric molecular weight ratio \ce{\text{MW}(\ce{Ca(OH)2})/\text{MW}(\ce{Mg}) = 74.09/24.305}, multiplied by an operational excess factor of 1.2 to account for reaction inefficiencies. The key reaction is \ce{\text{Mg}^{2+} + \ce{Ca(OH)2} \rightarrow \ce{Mg(OH)2(s)} + \text{Ca}^{2+}}; precipitated \ce{Mg(OH)2} reports to the solids phase, while the calcium introduced by the lime remains in solution and must be subsequently removed.
	\item[\textbullet\enskip Soda ash \ce{Na2CO3} targeting Ca (calcium introduced by lime)] at a ratio of 3.173 kg \ce{Na2CO3} per kg Ca introduced by the lime addition that targeted magnesium removal, with 90\% elimination of Ca. This is derived from MW(\ce{Na2CO3})/MW(Mg) $\times 1.2 \times 1.2 = 105.988/24.305 \times 1.44$, accounting for the stoichiometric equivalence of Ca introduced per Mg removed and two excess factors. The reaction is \ce{\text{Ca}^{2+} + \ce{Na2CO3} \rightarrow \ce{CaCO3(s)} + 2\,\text{Na}^+}.
	\item[\textbullet\enskip Soda ash \ce{Na2CO3} targeting Mg (as a secondary Ca removal dose)] at a ratio of 6.279 kg \ce{Na2CO3} per kg Mg, with 0\% elimination of Mg (this dose targets the calcium natively present in the brine, not the calcium introduced by lime). This is derived from MW(\ce{Na2CO3})/MW(Ca) $\times 1.2 = 105.988/40.078 \times 1.2$.
\end{description}

No coproducts are generated at this step; precipitated solids remain suspended in the treated slurry and are separated in the subsequent step. This step uses the \texttt{batch\_treatment\_generic} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-batchtreat}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Separation.}\quad

The separation step clarifies the treated slurry by removing precipitated \ce{Mg(OH)2} and \ce{CaCO3} solids, yielding a purified lithium-bearing solution (\texttt{pls\_post\_polishing}) suitable for downstream lithium carbonate precipitation. The primary input is \texttt{treated\_slurry} in liters, converted to the step basis (\texttt{mixture\_being\_separated} in m\textsuperscript{3}/h) via an IPCF of $10^{-3}$ (L to m\textsuperscript{3}). The CCF is $1.25 \times 10^{-6}$ kg/L, consistent with the upstream step. The output \texttt{pls\_post\_polishing} has a POCF of 1000 (m\textsuperscript{3} to L) and a yield rate of 0.995, reflecting a 0.5\% lithium loss to solids during filtration.

One reagent is consumed: a filter aid (precoat, e.g.\ diatomaceous earth) dosed at 0.005 kg per m\textsuperscript{3} of treated slurry against the step basis. This value is a rough engineering estimate; a suitable published reference for diatomaceous earth dosing in mineral brine clarification has not been identified and represents a low-priority refinement.

The residue coproduct is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_35} sink, representing an on-site filter cake at approximately 35\% solids fraction. The coproduct conversion factor of 0.232 m\textsuperscript{3} wet solids per m\textsuperscript{3} treated slurry is derived from first-principles stoichiometry. The batch treatment reagent doses (3.658 kg \ce{Ca(OH)2} per kg Mg at 95\% removal; 3.173 kg \ce{Na2CO3} per kg Ca at 90\% removal) yield 36.9 g/L \ce{Mg(OH)2} and 72.8 g/L \ce{CaCO3} dry precipitate at the concentrated brine chemistry (Mg: 12,960 ppm; Ca: 1,535 ppm; $\rho$ = 1.25 kg/L), totaling 109.7 g/L dry solids. At a filter press cake of 35 wt\% solids and wet density of 1.35 g/cm\textsuperscript{3}, this yields 0.232 m\textsuperscript{3} wet cake per m\textsuperscript{3} slurry. This estimate is cross-validated against Silver Peak's disclosed lime consumption of 1.3 t \ce{CaO} per tonne \ce{Li2CO3}. The residue composition is assumed to be as 25\% \ce{Mg(OH)2}, 25\% \ce{CaCO3}, and 50\% water by volume. We assume no volume loss here, as water lost is replenished from water from tailings slurry dewatering and recycling from downstream lithium carbonate production steps, though a full water balance is a key future area for improvement. 

This step uses the \texttt{thickener\_generic} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-thickener}).

\paragraph{Clay Comminution and Acid Leach}\label{app:04-methods-facilitychoices-clayleach}
Clay-based lithium sources allow for relatively straightforward chemical extraction from fine-grained matrices, and comminution serves primarily to control input particle sizes. Even weakly bound sediments, as in projects such as Rhyolite Ridge, Tonopah Flats, and Bonnie Claire, are typically delivered from the mine as run-of-mine fragments and therefore require simple primary crushing for size control prior to slurry preparation \citep{khakmardanLifeCycleAssessment2024, mousavinezhadNextLithiumBoom2025}. For harder claystone systems such as Thacker Pass, Clayton Valley, and TLC, additional mechanical processing, modeled here as attrition scrubbing, is required to break down aggregated particles prior to leaching.

Classification and screening are key operations in clay flowsheets to control slurry particle size and ensure consistent leaching performance. Rather than specifying individual screening or hydrocyclone devices, these operations are abstracted into a generic classifier block parameterized by cut size and efficiency. Given the importance of water management in Nevada-based claystone systems, a thickening step is included following classification to regulate solids concentration and recycle process water.

Clay-origin slurries are processed with sulfuric acid leaching, after which the leached slurry undergoes multistage solid--liquid separation, typically represented as a counter-current decantation (CCD) system, to recover pregnant leach solution and minimize soluble lithium losses to residue. Water recovery and solution concentration are key drivers at this stage and materially affect both reagent consumption and downstream purification requirements. Staged neutralization and impurity removal steps are required to control iron, aluminum, magnesium, calcium, and residual acidity prior to lithium recovery. While specific precipitation sequences vary across project disclosures, these operations perform the same functional role: conditioning the sulfate solution to within acceptable impurity tolerances. The resulting purified lithium sulfate solution is then processed into lithium carbonate.

Of note is that the last three steps of this process are not included in the Thacker Pass project flowsheet, where a magnesium recycling route was adopted in which magnesium salts are removed via evaporative crystallization -- consuming fewer reagents at the cost of substantially higher thermal energy demand. Our generic model instead fully precipitates all magnesium with quicklime, causing total quicklime consumption to reach approximately 9,200 kg \ce{CaO}/t \ce{Li2CO3}, compared to the disclosed 2,630 kg/t. This addition of quicklime also necessitates addition of sodium carbonate to polish the additional calcium, significantly increasing costs.

This facility is composed of the following blocks:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Crushing:}] Primary crushing for particle size control.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Attrition Scrubbing:}] Attrition scrubbing for harder claystone systems.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Classification:}] Classification and screening for slurry particle size control.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Thickening:}] Thickening to regulate solids concentration and recycle process water.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Acid Leaching:}] Sulfuric acid leaching of classified clay slurry.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Neutralization:}] Staged neutralization, removing Fe, Al, and others, via pH adjustment and precipitation. 
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Multistage Primary Counter-Current Decantation:}] Primary counter-current decantation for pregnant leach solution recovery.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Impurity Removal:}] 
    Chemical removal of impurities (Mg, Ca, etc.) via pH adjustment and precipitation
\end{description}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Crushing.}\quad

The crushing step reduces run-of-mine (\texttt{rom\_ore\_feed}) claystone to a particle size suitable for slurry preparation and attrition scrubbing. The primary input is ROM ore in m\textsuperscript{3}, converted to the step basis of \texttt{crushed\_ore\_throughput} in tonnes via an IPCF of 1.9, reflecting the in-situ bulk density of Thacker Pass hectorite clay (approximately 1.9 t/m\textsuperscript{3})~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}; this value should be loaded dynamically from project-specific data for other clay projects rather than hardcoded. The step CCF is 0.001 kg ore per (tonne-ppm), the standard conversion for mass-basis ppm constituents. Only Li is tracked as a constituent through the mining and comminution circuit, sufficient to propagate the ore grade forward to sizing calculations in downstream steps.

No reagents are consumed at this step. A \texttt{rejected\_coarse} coproduct stream routed to the \texttt{tailings\_solid} sink is defined in the data schema with a conversion factor of zero, reflecting the modeling assumption that no coarse material is rejected to waste at the crushing stage. Oversize material is instead recirculated within the crushing circuit and appears in the feed to attrition scrubbing in the following step.

This step uses the \texttt{crushing\_cycle} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-crushing}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Attrition Scrubbing.}\quad

The attrition scrubbing step deagglomerates crushed claystone particles and produces a pumpable slurry at a target solids concentration suitable for classification. The primary input is \texttt{crushed\_ore} in tonnes, converted to the step basis (\texttt{scrubbed\_slurry\_throughput}, in m\textsuperscript{3} of slurry at approximately 30 wt\% solids) via an IPCF of 3.40. This factor accounts for both the mass-to-volume conversion of crushed ore into a slurry and an assumed 20\% contribution of coarse recycled material from the downstream classification step: for a target slurry at 30 wt\% solids using a grain density of 2.6 t/m\textsuperscript{3} and water density of 1.0 t/m\textsuperscript{3}, the mixture density is $\rho_{\text{mix}} = 1/(w_s/\rho_s + (1-w_s)/\rho_w)$, giving a step CCF of $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3 \approx 3.68 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm. Including the 20\% recycle flow from classification yields the effective IPCF of approximately 3.40.

The output \texttt{scrubbed\_slurry} is a diluted slurry at approximately 20 wt\% solids, reflecting water addition during scrubbing. The POCF of 1.613 converts from the 30 wt\% step basis to the 20 wt\% output basis, derived as the ratio of the inverse CCF values at the two solids fractions: $\text{POCF} = (1/(0.2 \cdot \rho_{20\%})) / (1/(0.3 \cdot \rho_{30\%}))$. The output CCF is correspondingly $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3 \approx 2.28 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm at 20 wt\% solids.

Two reagents are dosed against the step basis volume: a slurry dispersant at 0.001 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of slurry (a rough engineering estimate for clay/silt deflocculation, with no constituent elimination), and makeup process water at 0.4 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of slurry. The process water ratio reflects new water introduced for slurrying, with the remainder of total water demand (approximately 73 m\textsuperscript{3} per 100 m\textsuperscript{3} of scrubbed slurry output) assumed to be supplied by recycled thickener overflow and other internal streams not explicitly tracked in the current model. No coproducts are generated at this step.

This step uses the \texttt{attrition\_scrubber} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-attritionscrubber}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Classification.}\quad
The classification step separates fine-grained particles suitable for acid leaching from coarser material that is recycled back to attrition scrubbing, abstracting the function of hydrocyclones and/or screens into a single sizing operation. The primary input is \texttt{scrubbed\_slurry} at 20 wt\% solids, with an IPCF of 1.0 (the step basis is the same volumetric unit as the incoming slurry) and a CCF of $2.28 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm, consistent with the output of the preceding step.

The output \texttt{classified\_slurry} is diluted to approximately 15 wt\% solids by the classification circuit, with a POCF of 1.380 converting from the 20 wt\% step basis to the 15 wt\% output, and an output CCF of $1.65 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm. These CCFs are derived using the same mixture density formulation as the attrition scrubbing step, with grain density of 2.6 t/m\textsuperscript{3}: $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$.

The yield rate is set to 0.95, reflecting a 5\% lithium loss at this stage attributable to fine particle entrainment in coarse oversize streams. Under a fully closed recycle of coarse material back to attrition scrubbing, the theoretical yield would be 1.0; the 0.95 value represents a practical allowance for incomplete recirculation and mineralogical losses. Note that a total of 8\% lithium loss is attributed to coarse gangue mineralisation in the Thacker Pass disclosure~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}; this loss is spread over several steps. No reagents are consumed and no coproducts are generated at this step; coarse oversize returns to \texttt{scrubbing} as part of the recycle loop described above.

This step uses the \texttt{classifier\_generic} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-classifier}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Thickening.}\quad

The thickening step concentrates the classified slurry from approximately 15 wt\% solids to approximately 55 wt\% solids, preparing a dense leach feed for the acid leaching circuit and recovering process water in the thickener overflow for recycle. The primary input is \texttt{classified\_slurry} with an IPCF of 1.0 and an input CCF of $1.65 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm, consistent with the output of the classification step ($w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$ at 15 wt\% solids, grain density 2.6 t/m\textsuperscript{3}).

The output \texttt{thick\_leach\_feed} is at approximately 55 wt\% solids, with a POCF of 0.199 converting from the 15 wt\% step basis to the 55 wt\% output, derived as the ratio of inverse CCFs at the two solids fractions. The output CCF is $8.31 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm at 55 wt\% solids.

One reagent is consumed: a polymer flocculant dosed at $2.6 \times 10^{-4}$ kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of slurry feed against the step basis, with no constituent elimination. This dosage is calibrated to match the reported combined flocculant consumption across the thickener and decanter circuits in the Thacker Pass disclosure of approximately 260 g/t \cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}. The thickener overflow (\texttt{thickener\_overflow}) is defined as a coproduct with a conversion factor of zero, routed to the \texttt{wastewater\_treatment} sink to specify that water is assumed to be recycled back into the scrubbing and classification circuit. 

This step uses the \texttt{thickener\_generic} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-thickener}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Acid Leaching.}\quad

The acid leaching step dissolves lithium and other soluble species from the thickened claystone slurry using sulfuric acid, producing a leach slurry (\texttt{leached\_slurry}) containing a pregnant leach solution rich in \ce{Li2SO4} alongside gangue residue. The primary input is \texttt{thick\_leach\_feed} at approximately 55 wt\% solids, converted to the step basis (\texttt{leach\_slurry\_throughput} at approximately 34 wt\% solids) via an IPCF of 1.934, reflecting the dilution of the thickened feed upon addition of acid solution and makeup water. This IPCF is derived as the ratio of the inverse CCFs at 55 wt\% and 34 wt\% solids: $(1/(0.34 \cdot \rho_{\text{34\%}})) / (1/(0.55 \cdot \rho_{\text{55\%}}))$, using grain density 2.6 t/m\textsuperscript{3} throughout. The input CCF is $8.31 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm (at 55 wt\% solids), while the step and output CCF is $4.30 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm at 34 wt\% solids.

The principal reaction is the dissolution of lithium from the clay lattice:
\vspace{-1em}
\alteq{Lithium oxide combined with silicon dioxide and water in solid form reacts with aqueous sulfuric acid to produce aqueous lithium sulfate, solid silicon dioxide, and water.}
\begin{equation}
\ce{Li2O \cdot nSiO2 \cdot mH2O (s) + H2SO4 (aq) -> Li2SO4 (aq) + nSiO2 (s) + (m+1)H2O}
\end{equation}
\alteqend

Other gangue elements (e.g. Al, Mg, Ca, Fe) form soluble sulfates or secondary solids; their removal is handled in downstream impurity removal steps.  

Two reagents are dosed against the step basis:
\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
\item[\textbullet\enskip Sulfuric acid] at 85 kg \ce{H2SO4}/m\textsuperscript{3} leach slurry against the step basis volume. This dosage is calibrated to match the disclosure-reported consumption rate of approximately 16 kg acid per tonne \ce{Li2CO3}~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024, rothFeasibilityStudyNational2022}, with no constituent elimination (acid consumption drives dissolution but the Li constituent is tracked separately via yield rate).
\item[\textbullet\enskip Process water] at 0.483 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry against the step basis, representing the additional dilution water needed to transition from the 55 wt\% thickened feed to the 34 wt\% leach slurry operating density.
\end{description}
The output yield rate is 0.90, reflecting a 10\% lithium loss to the solid residue during leaching, consistent with the disclosed leaching recovery range of 85--90\%~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}. No coproducts are generated at this step; solid gangue residue remains entrained in the leach slurry and is separated in the subsequent neutralization and CCD circuit.

This step uses the \texttt{acid\_leach\_circuit} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-acidleach}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Neutralization.}\quad

The neutralization step consumes residual free acid in the leached slurry and initiates precipitation of iron and aluminum impurities prior to solid--liquid separation in the CCD circuit. The primary input is \texttt{leached\_slurry} at approximately 34 wt\% solids, with the input, step, and output CCFs all equal to $4.30 \times 10^{-4}$ kg ore per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), computed as $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$ (at 34 wt\% solids and grain density 2.6 t/m\textsuperscript{3}). 

Two reagents are dosed against the step basis:
\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
\item[\textbullet\enskip Limestone (\ce{CaCO3})] at 11.2 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry for bulk acid neutralization to pH $\sim$3.5, calibrated to match the reported limestone consumption rate of approximately 2.95 t/t \ce{Li2CO3} in the Thacker Pass disclosure~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}. The addition of limestone is assumed to not significantly change the throughput volume. No constituent elimination is modeled at this step --- precipitated \ce{Fe(OH)3} and gypsum remain in the slurry and are separated in the CCD circuit. 
\item[\textbullet\enskip Flocculant] at 0.03 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry, as a settling aid for the nascent precipitates ahead of the CCD. This value is a rough estimate.
\end{description}

The output \texttt{neutralized\_pls} passes to the CCD circuit with a POCF of 1.0 and a yield of 1.0.

This step uses the \texttt{staged\_neutralization} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-neutralization}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Counter-Current Decantation.}\quad

The Counter-Current Decantation (CCD) step separates the pregnant leach solution from the solid leach residue through multi-stage counter-current decantation, recovering dissolved lithium from entrained solution while minimizing lithium losses to the filter cake. The primary input is \texttt{neutralized\_pls} at approximately 34 wt\% solids, with input and step CCFs of $4.30 \times 10^{-4}$ kg solids per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm, consistent with the neutralization step output.

The output \texttt{washed\_pregnant\_leach\_solution} has a POCF of 1.31, reflecting the volumetric expansion from a 34 wt\% solids slurry feed to a clarified PLS at approximately 75 g/L total dissolved solids (TDS) and density $\sim$1.05 t/m\textsuperscript{3}. This factor accounts for the wash water introduced across the CCD stages and the liquid retained in the filter cake underflow; at a wash ratio in the range 0.75--1.25, the central estimate of 1.31 corresponds to approximately 99\% lithium recovery to solution~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}. The output CCF shifts to $1.05 \times 10^{-3}$ kg per m\textsuperscript{3} per ppm, reflecting the transition from a solids-containing slurry basis to a PLS density basis ($\rho_{\text{PLS}} / 10^3$). The yield rate is 0.99, consistent with the reported 0.5--1.5\% lithium loss to the leach residue~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}.

One reagent is consumed: flocculant at 0.04 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of CCD feed. This value is a rough estimate; typical polymer flocculant doses in CCD circuits for sulfate leach slurries range from approximately 20--80 g/t of solids processed~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}, and the modeled dose is broadly consistent with the lower end of this range given the solids loading implied by the 34 wt\% feed. Wash water addition across the CCD stages is implicit in the POCF of 1.31 but is not modeled as an explicit process water reagent input, on the assumption that wash water requirements are met by internal recycle streams from tailings and other sources. This assumption has not been verified against a closed water balance, and represents a priority refinement alongside the broader water recycling improvements noted in the main body Limitations section (\ref{sec:04-limitations}).

The filter cake coproduct (\texttt{filter\_cake}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_65} sink with a conversion factor of 0.3, representing approximately 0.3 m\textsuperscript{3} of dewatered cake per m\textsuperscript{3} of CCD feed. This fraction also warrants re-estimation against a water balance calculation once water recycling is explicitly modeled, as it is sensitive to the assumed wash ratio and cake moisture content.

This step uses the \texttt{multi\_stage\_ccd\_thickening} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-ccd}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Impurity Removal.}\quad

The impurity removal step precipitates dissolved magnesium and calcium from the washed PLS, producing a clarified lithium sulfate solution (\texttt{pls\_post\_polishing}) suitable for soda ash precipitation of lithium carbonate. This step represents the most significant divergence from the Thacker Pass project-specific flowsheet, which uses magnesium crystallization, ion exchange, and zero-liquid-discharge evaporation rather than chemical precipitation, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:04-scopemethod}. As such, the reagent doses, sludge volumes, and cost implications of this generic all-chemical route are approximate but conservative, and thus should be revisited with a more detailed mass balance in future work.

The primary input is \texttt{washed\_pregnant\_leach\_solution} at approximately 75~g/L total dissolved solids (TDS) and density $\rho_\text{PLS} \approx 1.05$~t/m\textsuperscript{3}. The input, step, and output CCFs are all $\rho_\text{PLS} \times 10^{-3} = 1.05 \times 10^{-3}$~kg/(m\textsuperscript{3}$\cdot$ppm), converting lithium concentration in ppm (mg~Li/kg~PLS) to kg~Li per m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS. The POCF is 1000, converting from the m\textsuperscript{3} PLS step basis to the litre output units of \texttt{pls\_post\_polishing}. The yield rate is 0.99, reflecting approximately 1\% lithium co-precipitation loss with the Mg/Ca sludge.

Two reagents drive the impurity removal chemistry; both are dosed volumetrically against the step basis (m\textsuperscript{3} PLS):

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]

\item[\textbullet\enskip Lime (\ce{CaO})] at 29.1 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} PLS for magnesium removal. The PLS contains approximately 11.1 g/L dissolved Mg (as \ce{MgSO4}), derived from the acid dissolution of Mg-bearing clay at a 20\% reactive ore fraction. Lime reacts with dissolved \ce{MgSO4} to precipitate \ce{Mg(OH)2}, with \ce{CaSO4} (gypsum) as a solid byproduct:
%
\begin{equation*}
  \ce{MgSO4 + CaO + H2O -> Mg(OH)2 v + CaSO4 v}
\end{equation*}
%
The stoichiometric minimum for Mg removal alone is $11.1 \times (56/24.3) = 25.6$ kg \ce{CaO}/m\textsuperscript{3}; the modeled dose of 29.1 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} includes an approximate allowance for residual iron and aluminum not fully removed upstream, as well as general reagent excess. This corresponds to approximately 9,200 kg \ce{CaO}/t \ce{Li2CO3}, substantially exceeding the Thacker Pass disclosed consumption of 2,630 kg/t \cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}, which reflects their magnesium crystallization route.

\item[\textbullet\enskip Soda ash (\ce{Na2CO3})] at 5.3 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} PLS for calcium polishing. The lime addition introduces calcium into the system, most of which co-precipitates as gypsum. The fraction that remains dissolved (approximately 2 g/L, limited by gypsum solubility) must be removed to avoid contaminating the downstream \ce{Li2CO3} product. Soda ash precipitates this residual calcium as \ce{CaCO3}:
\alteq{Aqueous calcium ion reacts with sodium carbonate to produce calcium carbonate precipitate and two sodium ions.}
\begin{equation*}
  \ce{Ca^{2+} + Na2CO3 -> CaCO3 v + 2Na^+}
\end{equation*}
\alteqend
At $2.0 \times (106/40) = 5.3$ kg/m\textsuperscript{3}, this dose is approximately stoichiometric for the dissolved calcium at gypsum saturation.
 
\item[\textbullet\enskip Flocculant] at 0.1 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} PLS as a settling aid. This is a rough estimate.

\end{description}

The impurity sludge coproduct (\texttt{impurity\_sludge}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_25} sink. The conversion factor of 0.05 m\textsuperscript{3} wet sludge per m\textsuperscript{3} PLS feed is a rough placeholder; this conversion factor should be revisited.

This step uses the \texttt{staged\_neutralization} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-neutralization}); the same generic machine block is used for both the neutralization and impurity removal steps, as both involve reagent addition to a slurry in agitated tanks. 

\paragraph{Lepidolite Comminution and Processing}\label{app:04-methods-facilitychoices-lepidproc}

Our model is based primarily on the methods described in \citet{dengSystematicReviewSulfate2026} and \citet{yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012}, as the sulfate roasting--water leach pathway is identified as the most common approach for processing lepidolite \citep{gaoLithiumExtractionHard2023, parkEffectiveLithiumExtraction2023, nanMethodsExtractingLithium2019, nanMethodsExtractingLithium2022, zouMethodEfficientLithium2024}.

Hard rock ores such as lepidolite require mechanical size reduction and beneficiation prior to chemical processing. Run-of-mine lepidolite material is first crushed to approximately 150--250 $\mu$m, classified by size, and then beneficiated with froth flotation. The flotation step is critical for this pathway: at typical Jiangxi ore grades of less than 0.4\% \ce{Li2O} ($\sim$ 1,860 ppm Li), direct roasting of ROM material would require processing enormous reagent and energy quantities per unit of lithium product. Froth flotation using amine collectors and phosphate depressants selectively recovers lepidolite minerals into a concentrate at approximately 1.6\% \ce{Li2O} ($\sim$ 7,600 ppm Li)~\cite{garrettHandbookLithiumNatural2004, jiaoKeyTechnologiesDevelopment2024}, reducing the mass throughput by approximately 60\%~\citep{jiaoKeyTechnologiesDevelopment2024}. After flotation, the throughput is ground to a product size of approximately 50--75 $\mu$m. This latter grinding step uses high-energy mechanical activation milling, which introduces structural defects in the mica lattice to facilitate the solid-state reactions during subsequent roasting. This product is then dewatered prior to entering the roasting circuit.

Following comminution, lepidolite is processed via a sulfate roasting--water leach pathway. Due to the significant presence of fluorine, acid-, chlorine-, or autoclave-based methods can produce corrosive mixtures and/or toxic gases; sulfate-based methods instead allow for the formation of precipitated fluorides (e.g., \ce{CaF2}, \ce{AlF3}), at the expense of more costly sulfate reagents. The roasting step, conducted at approximately 800--950$^\circ$C, converts lithium in tightly bound mica lattices to water-soluble lithium sulfate. The resulting calcine is then subjected to water leaching, producing a pregnant leach solution containing lithium, other dissolved metals, and residual sulfate species. Impurities are removed through staged neutralization and pH adjustment to precipitate aluminum, iron, magnesium, and other species. Solid--liquid separation then yields a clarified lithium solution suitable for downstream carbonate precipitation via soda ash addition.

This facility is composed of the following blocks:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Crushing:}] Primary and secondary size reduction.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Classification and Flotation:}] Classification, screening, and flotation for lepidolite concentration.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Grinding:}] Mechanical activation milling using a high-energy ball mill grinder to target product size.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Dewatering:}] Dewatering of activated concentrate prior to roasting.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Sulfate Roasting:}] Sulfate roasting at 800--950$^\circ$C.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Water Leaching:}] Water leaching of calcine to produce pregnant leach solution.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Impurity Removal and Neutralization:}] Staged neutralization and pH adjustment for impurity 
    precipitation.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Clarification:}] Clarification of lithium solution for 
    downstream processing.
\end{description}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Crushing.}\quad

The crushing step reduces ROM lepidolite ore (\texttt{rom\_ore\_feed}) from run-of-mine fragments to a particle size of approximately 150--250~$\mu$ suitable for wet classification and flotation. The primary input is ROM ore in m\textsuperscript{3}, converted to the step basis of \texttt{crushed\_ore\_throughput} in tonnes via an IPCF of 2.7, reflecting the grain density of lepidolite (2.7 t/m\textsuperscript{3}). The step CCF is $1.0 \times 10^{-3}$, and the input CCF is $2.7 \times 10^{-3}$ kg constituent per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), reflecting the conversion from volumetric input to mass-basis step. Only Li is tracked as a constituent through the comminution circuit. No reagents are consumed. The output \texttt{crushed\_ore} passes to classification with a POCF of 1.0 and a yield rate of 0.99, reflecting a 1\% loss to fines and dust during crushing.

This step uses the \texttt{crushing\_cycle} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-crushing}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Classification \& Flotation.}\quad

The classification and flotation step separates fine lepidolite particles from coarser gangue and produces a lepidolite concentrate enriched in lithium, which is the key beneficiation step in this pathway. The step is constituent-driven: the volume-defining basis is the Li constituent of the input stream, meaning that the required flotation circuit volume scales with the lithium mass to be processed rather than with bulk ore throughput. This correctly captures the dependence of flotation circuit sizing on ore grade.

The primary input is \texttt{crushed\_ore} in tonnes is converted to the step basis (\texttt{classified\_slurry\_throughput} in m\textsuperscript{3} of slurry at approximately 30 wt\% solids) via an IPCF of 2.7037, derived as $1/(w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}})$ at 30 wt\% solids with grain density 2.7 t/m\textsuperscript{3}: $\rho_{\text{mix}} = 1/(0.3/2.7 + 0.7/1.0) = 1.233$ t/m\textsuperscript{3}, giving $1/(0.3 \times 1.233) = 2.704$ The step CCF is $3.70 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), computed as $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$.

The output \texttt{classified\_slurry} is a lepidolite concentrate at approximately 60 wt\% solids, with a POCF of 0.3836 converting from the 30 wt\% step basis to the 60 wt\% concentrate, using the density mixing formula. The output CCF is $9.64 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (\si{m^3} $\cdot$ ppm), derived as $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}}/10^3$ at 60 wt\% solids. The yield rate is 0.70, representing the combined lithium recovery across classification and flotation, decomposed as \[r_{\text{combined}} = r_{\text{class}} \times r_{\text{flot}} = 0.93 \times 0.75 = 0.70\] where $r_{\text{class}} = 0.93$ reflects a 7\% lithium loss to classification slimes, consistent with the 5--8\% range reported for Jiangxi industrial lepidolite desliming operations \cite{zhangInnovativeRecoveryLepidolite2025}, and $r_{\text{flot}} = 0.75$ reflects flotation lithium recovery at the conservative end of the 75--82\% range reported for industrial-scale desliming--flotation circuits processing low-grade Jiangxi ore \cite{jiaoKeyTechnologiesDevelopment2024, zhangInnovativeRecoveryLepidolite2025}. The remaining 30\% of lithium reports to the coarse reject stream (approximately 7\% via classification slimes and 23\% via flotation tailings). This step performs the key concentration from approximately 1,860 ppm Li (0.4\% \ce{Li2O}) at ROM to approximately 7,430 ppm Li ($\sim$1.6\% \ce{Li2O}) in the concentrate --- a 4.0
$\times$ grade concentration at 70\% combined recovery, yielding a ROM-to-concentrate mass ratio of approximately 5.7:1.

Three reagents are dosed against the step basis volume: process water at 0.863 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} for slurrying and flotation circuit operation; depressants (sodium hexametaphosphate) at 0.1 kg/m\textsuperscript{3}, which suppress non-lepidolite silicate minerals to improve selectivity; and collectors (dodecylamine) at 0.12 kg/m\textsuperscript{3}, which selectively adsorb onto lepidolite surfaces to render them hydrophobic and recoverable by froth flotation~\cite{jiaoKeyTechnologiesDevelopment2024}. None of these reagents eliminate tracked constituents from the process stream --- their function is physical separation rather than chemical removal.

Two coproducts are generated. The coarse reject (\texttt{coarse\_reject}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_65} sink with a conversion factor of 0.75 \si{m^3/m^3} of slurry, representing the 82.5\% of feed solids and gangue that report to classification slimes and flotation tailings; this large fraction is consistent with the low ROM ore grade and the $\sim 5.7 \times$ concentration ratio achieved. Thickener overflow (\texttt{thickener\_overflow}) is routed to wastewater treatment and is assumed to be recycled internally, but this needs to be validated with a full water balance (\ref{sec:04-limitations}). 

This step uses the \texttt{classifier\_flotation} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-classifierflotation}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Grinding.}\quad

The grinding step reduces the lepidolite concentrate to a fine product size of approximately 50--75 $\mu$ via high-energy mechanical activation milling, breaking down the mica crystal structure to yield an activated concentrate that is reactive in subsequent sulfate roasting. This mechanical activation is a distinguishing feature of the lepidolite pathway --- the fine grinding increases the surface area and introduces structural defects in the mica lattice that facilitate the solid-state reactions during roasting~\citep{dengSystematicReviewSulfate2026, yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012}.

The primary input is \texttt{classified\_slurry} at 60 wt\% solids, with an IPCF of 1.0 (the step basis is the same volumetric unit). The step and input CCFs are both 0.0009643 kg solids per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), consistent with the 60 wt\% slurry density basis. No reagents are consumed. The output \texttt{ground\_slurry} has a POCF of 1.0 and a yield rate of 0.995, reflecting a 0.5\% loss during milling. No coproducts are generated.

This step uses the \texttt{high\_energy\_grinding\_mill} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-hegm}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Dewatering.}\quad

The dewatering step removes water from the ground lepidolite concentrate slurry to produce a filter cake suitable for high-temperature sulfate roasting. Roasting requires a low-moisture feed; excess water would consume thermal energy in evaporation rather than driving the sulfate activation reactions, and could also cause handling problems in the roasting train. The primary input is \texttt{ground\_slurry} at 60 wt\% solids, with input and step CCFs of $9.64 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$
ppm), derived as $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$ at 60 wt\% solids with grain density 2.7 t/m\textsuperscript{3}: $\rho_{\text{mix}} = 1/(0.6/2.7 + 0.4/1.0) = 1.607$ t/m\textsuperscript{3}.

The output \texttt{roaster\_feed} is an 80 wt\% solids filter cake, expressed in tonnes rather than m\textsuperscript{3}, with a POCF of 1.205 converting from m\textsuperscript{3} of 60 wt\% slurry to tonnes of 80 wt\% cake: $\text{POCF} = w_{s,\text{in}} \cdot \rho_{\text{mix,in}} / w_{s,\text{out}} = 0.6 \times 1.607 / 0.8$. The output CCF is $8.0 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (tonne $\cdot$ ppm), reflecting the mass basis of the cake: $w_{s,\text{cake}} / 10^3 = 0.8 / 10^3$. No reagents are consumed. Wash liquor (\texttt{wash\_liquor}) is generated at approximately 0.4 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of input slurry, routed to the \texttt{wastewater\_treatment} sink; this represents approximately 0.4 t/m\textsuperscript{3} of water removed during pressing, consistent with the reduction in water content from 0.643 t/m\textsuperscript{3} at 60 wt\% solids to 0.241 t/m\textsuperscript{3} at 80 wt\% solids. Yield rate is 1.0.

This step uses the \texttt{mechanical\_dewatering} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-dewater}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Sulfate Roasting.}\quad

The sulfate roasting step thermally activates the dewatered lepidolite concentrate by reacting it with sodium sulfate, potassium sulfate, and lime at 800--950$^\circ$
C, converting lithium from the tightly bound mica lattice into water-soluble lithium sulfate. The central reaction is:
\alteq{Solid lepidolite consisting of lithium oxide, aluminum oxide, three silicon dioxide units, and potassium fluoride reacts with sodium sulfate to produce solid lithium sulfate, solid aluminum oxide, solid silicon dioxide, and potassium fluoride as a gas or solid.}
\begin{equation}
	\ce{(Li2O)(Al2O3)(3SiO2)(KF)(s) + Na2SO4 -> Li2SO4 (s) + Al2O3 (s) + 3SiO2 (s) + KF (g/s)}
\end{equation}
\alteqend
with fluorine species captured by lime addition as \ce{CaF2} and \ce{AlF3} precipitates rather than volatilizing as toxic \ce{HF} gas.

The primary input is \texttt{roaster\_feed} in tonnes of 80 wt\% solids cake, with an IPCF of 1.6, representing the total roasting mix mass per tonne of input cake after addition of all reagents and residual moisture. This factor accounts for: dry ore solids (0.8 t per tonne cake), sodium sulfate addition (0.5 $\times$ 0.8 = 0.4 t), lime addition (0.15 $\times$ 0.8 = 0.12 t), potassium sulfate addition (0.1 $\times$ 0.8 = 0.08 t), and residual moisture (0.2 t). The input and step CCF are both $5.0 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (tonne $\cdot$ ppm): $w_{s,\text{cake}} / (G_{18} \times 10^3) = 0.8 / (1.6 \times 10^3)$.

Three reagents are dosed against the \texttt{roasting\_mix} step basis:
\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
\item[\textbullet\enskip Sodium sulfate (\ce{Na2SO4})] at 250 kg/tonne roasting mix (equivalent to 0.5 t per tonne dry lepidolite concentrate), derived from \citet{yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012}.
\item[\textbullet\enskip Lime (\ce{CaO})] at 75 kg/tonne roasting mix (equivalent to 0.15 t per tonne dry concentrate). The base value of 0.1 t/t from \citet{yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012} is increased by 50\% following \citet{luongFactorsAffectingExtraction2013} to ensure complete fluoride capture, suppressing \ce{HF} volatilization.
\item[\textbullet\enskip Potassium sulfate (\ce{K2SO4})] at 50 kg/tonne roasting mix (equivalent to 0.1 t per tonne dry concentrate), derived from \citet{yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012}, to supplement the alkali sulfate flux and improve lithium extraction efficiency.
\end{description}
The output \texttt{roasted\_mix} is the calcine after roasting, with water evaporated during the process. The POCF is 0.875, derived as the ratio of the calcine mass (excluding evaporated water: $0.8 \times (1 + 0.5 + 0.15 + 0.1) = 1.4$ t) to the total roasting mix mass (1.6 t). The output CCF is $6.06 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (tonne $\cdot$ ppm): $w_{s,\text{cake}} / (0.8 \times (1 + 0.5 + 0.15) \times 10^3)$. A fluorine offgas coproduct (\texttt{fluorine\_offgas}) is defined with a conversion factor of zero, reflecting the assumption that lime additions during roasting fix essentially all fluorine as solid precipitates before volatilization.

This step uses the \texttt{sulfate\_roasting\_train\_lepidolite} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-sulfateroasting}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Water Leaching.}\quad

The water leaching step dissolves the lithium sulfate and other soluble species from the roasted calcine into aqueous solution, producing a pregnant leach solution for downstream impurity removal. The primary input is \texttt{roasted\_mix} in tonnes of calcine, converted to the step basis (\texttt{leaching\_slurry} at approximately 20 wt\% solids) via an IPCF of 4.370, derived as $1/(w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}})$ at 20 wt\% solids with assumed calcine grain density of 2.7 t/m\textsuperscript{3}: $\rho_{\text{mix}} = 1/(0.2/2.7 + 0.8/1.0) = 1.144$ t/m\textsuperscript{3}, giving $1/(0.2 \times 1.144) = 4.370$. The input CCF is $6.06 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (tonne $\cdot$ ppm), consistent with the roasting output. The step CCF is $1.51 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), derived as the product of the 20 wt\% slurry density ($w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3 = 0.229$ kg/L) and the mass fraction of original ore solids in the calcine ($0.8 / (0.8 + 0.265 + 0.15) = 0.659$), which corrects for the dilution of the ore-derived Li quantity by reagent-derived solids in the calcine.

Process water is dosed at 0.915 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry basis, derived from the water fraction required to produce a 20 wt\% slurry: $(1 - w_s) \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / \rho_{\text{water}} = 0.8 \times 1.144 / 1.0 = 0.915$ m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3}. The POCF is 0.869, computed as the process water ratio scaled by the 95\% yield ($0.915 \times 0.95$), converting from m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry to m\textsuperscript{3} of pregnant leach solution (\texttt{pregnant\_leach\_solution}). The output CCF is $2.29 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m\textsuperscript{3} $\cdot$ ppm), computed as $w_s \cdot \rho_{\text{mix}} / 10^3$ at 20 wt\% solids without the calcine composition correction, since the output is a PLS where Li concentration is referenced to total solution mass. The yield rate is 0.95, reflecting a 5\% lithium loss to the leach residue. The leach residue coproduct (\texttt{leach\_residue}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_65} sink with a conversion factor of 0.131 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of leach slurry, representing the solid fraction that does not enter solution; residue constituents include Al, Fe, Si, and \ce{CaF2}, which are classified as non-hazardous gangue. The assumption of calcine grain density equal to lepidolite density (2.7 t/m\textsuperscript{3}) is a simplification, as roasting modifies the mineralogical composition of the solids, but is considered acceptable for the purposes of volumetric sizing.

This step uses the \texttt{water\_leach} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-waterleach}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Impurity Removal and Neutralization.}\quad

The impurity removal step precipitates dissolved magnesium, calcium, aluminum, iron, and trace impurities from the pregnant leach solution via staged pH adjustment, producing a clarified lithium sulfate solution suitable for downstream carbonate precipitation. The primary input is \texttt{pregnant\_leach\_solution} at approximately 20 wt\% suspended solids, with input, step, and output CCFs all equal to $2.29 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m$^{3} \cdot$ppm), derived as $w_s \cdot \rho_\text{mix} / 10^3$ at 20 wt\% solids with assumed calcine grain density 2.7 t/m$^3$. This density approximation introduces some error since roasting modifies the mineralogy of the solids, but we assume it reasonable for volumetric sizing purposes.
 
Three reagents are dosed against the step basis volume:
 
\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
\item[\textbullet\enskip Neutralizing agent] at 4 kg/m$^3$ of PLS, modeled as a generic mixed reagent representing a combination of lime, limestone, and/or NaOH for staged pH adjustment to precipitate \ce{Fe(OH)3}, \ce{Al(OH)3}, and \ce{Mg(OH)2}. The dosage is a rough estimate: stoichiometric lime demand for Mg precipitation could be approximately 3--6 kg/m$^3$, but the actual demand is moderated by the lower impurity concentrations in a water leach PLS compared to a sulfuric acid leach, and by the use of a mixed reagent~\cite{yanExtractionLithiumLepidolite2012, dengSystematicReviewSulfate2026}. This uncertainty in both composition and neutralizing agent combination warrants further refinement against measured PLS impurity data.
\item[\textbullet\enskip Flocculant] at 0.005 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS as a settling aid. This is also a rough estimate.
\item[\textbullet\enskip Sulfuric acid] at 1 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS for pH re-adjustment after primary neutralization, to avoid overshooting the target pH and precipitating lithium-bearing compounds. This is also a rough estimate.
\end{description}

The output \texttt{neutralized\_pls} passes to the clarification step with a POCF of 1.0 and a yield rate of 0.95, representing approximately 5\% lithium loss to the impurity sludge and entrained liquor --- consistent with the total lithium losses across the magnesium crystallization and zero liquid discharge (ZLD) circuits reported in the Thacker Pass disclosure~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}, and applied here as a conservative proxy given the different process route. The impurity sludge coproduct (\texttt{impurity\_sludge}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_20} hazardous secure landfill sink with a conversion factor of 0.05 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS, estimated from the 5\% lithium yield loss as a proportional proxy for sludge volume generation.

This step uses the \texttt{staged\_neutralization} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-neutralization}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Clarification.}\quad

The clarification step performs a final solid--liquid separation on the neutralized PLS to remove residual precipitates and suspended solids, yielding a clarified lithium solution (\texttt{pls\_post\_polishing}) suitable for soda ash precipitation. The primary input is \texttt{neutralized\_pls}, with input and step CCFs of $2.29 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm), consistent with the upstream step. One reagent is consumed: flocculant at 0.005 kg/m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS, a rough estimate for polishing-stage clarification where precipitate loadings are low.
 
The output \texttt{pls\_post\_polishing} is expressed in liters, with a POCF of 1000 (m$^3$ to L) and an output CCF of $2.29 \times 10^{-7}$ kg constituent per (L$\cdot$ppm), derived by dividing the m$^3$-basis CCF by 1000. The yield rate is 0.995, reflecting a 0.5\% lithium loss to residual impurity sludge. The impurity sludge coproduct (\texttt{impurity\_sludge}) is routed to the \texttt{tailings\_20} hazardous landfill sink with a conversion factor of 0.005 m\textsuperscript{3}/m\textsuperscript{3} of PLS input, consistent with the small residual solids loading at this polishing stage.

This step uses the \texttt{solid\_liquid\_separation} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-sls}).

\paragraph{Pregnant Leach Solution to Lithium Carbonate (Common to All Pathways)}\label{app:04-methods-facilitychoices-pls}

This section applies to the conversion of purified lithium-bearing solution to lithium carbonate product \citep{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a, chordiaLifeCycleEnvironmental2022}. Although upstream chemistry differs between chloride-based brine pathways (\ce{LiCl}) and sulfate-based ore pathways (\ce{Li2SO4}), the downstream precipitation and solids handling configuration is broadly similar.

In both cases, lithium carbonate is produced by reaction of dissolved lithium with soda ash. Chloride systems generate sodium chloride as a byproduct while sulfate systems generate sodium sulfate; these differences primarily affect salt management and recycle strategy rather than equipment configuration. The core precipitation, thickening, washing, and drying sequence remains functionally consistent across pathways.

Precipitation is conducted at elevated temperatures to improve reaction kinetics and reduce lithium carbonate solubility. Thermal requirements are modeled generically, with pathway-specific parameterization to account for differences in reaction temperature, capturing energy demand without prescribing a specific crystallizer architecture. Following soda ash precipitation, lithium carbonate is separated from mother liquor via thickening and filtration. Washing and drying complete product finishing. Rather than specifying individual equipment types, these operations are represented as aggregated solids-handling steps parameterized by recovery efficiency, moisture content, and recycle fraction.

This facility is composed of the following blocks, with pathway-specific parameterization depending on whether the upstream pathway is chloride- or sulfate-based:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Lithium Carbonate Precipitation:}] Soda ash precipitation, including preheating and cooling.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Thickening and Dewatering:}] Thickening and dewatering of lithium carbonate solids.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Washing and Purification:}] Washing and purification, to remove residual Na, K, Mg. 
    \item[\textbullet\enskip\texttt{Drying and Packaging:}] Drying and packaging of final product.
\end{description}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Lithium Carbonate Precipitation.}\quad

Lithium carbonate is produced by reacting dissolved lithium with soda ash (\ce{Na2CO3}):
\begin{equation}
    2\,\text{Li}^+ + \ce{Na2CO3} \rightarrow \ce{Li2CO3(s)} + 2\,\text{Na}^+
\end{equation}
In chloride-based brine systems the byproduct is NaCl, whereas in sulfate-based ore systems it is \ce{Na2SO4}; these differences affect downstream salt management but not the precipitation equipment configuration. Both pathways use the same underlying production step, with pathway-specific parameterization for the input chemistry, soda ash dosage, and yield rate.
This step uses the \texttt{precipitator} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-precipitator}).

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Chloride pathway.}\quad
 
The primary input is \texttt{pls\_post\_polishing} (concentrated lithium brine) in liters, with the step constitutent-driven on the Li constituent such that the IPCF is 1.0 by definition --- the step volume scales with the ratio of lithium mass in output volume to input PLS, rather than the bulk PLS volume. The input and step CCFs are both $1.25 \times 10^{-6}$ kg Li per (L $\cdot$ ppm), reflecting the density of the concentrated PLS (1.25 kg/L) divided by $10^6$ to convert from ppm (mg/kg) to kg/L. The POCF is $10^{-3}$ (L to m$^3$). The output \texttt{li2co3\_slurry} is a 20 wt\% \ce{Li2CO3} slurry with density $\rho_\text{mix} = 1/(0.2/2.11 + 0.8/1.0) = 1.118$ t/m$^3$, giving an output CCF of $0.2 \times 1.118 / 10^3 = 2.24 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm). The yield rate is 0.97, reflecting a 3\% lithium loss to mother liquor and wash streams, consistent with reported losses from the precipitation circuit in the Silver Peak disclosure \cite{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a}.
 
Soda ash is dosed at 11.5 kg \ce{Na2CO3} per kg Li, targeting the Li constituent. This corresponds to 2.2 t \ce{Na2CO3} per tonne \ce{Li2CO3} produced, consistent with the ratio described in the flowsheet of the Silver Peak disclosure \cite{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a}.
 
\medskip
\noindent\textit{Sulfate pathway.}\quad
 
The primary input is \texttt{pls\_post\_polishing} in liters, with the step constituent-driven on the Li constituent and IPCF of 1.0 by definition. The input and step CCFs are both $2.29 \times 10^{-7}$ kg Li per (L$\cdot$ppm), derived as $w_s \cdot \rho_\text{mix} / 10^6$ at 20 wt\% solids with grain density 2.7 t/m$^3$: $\rho_\text{mix} = 1/(0.2/2.7 + 0.8/1.0) = 1.144$ t/m$^3$, giving $0.2 \times 1.144 / 10^6 = 2.29 \times 10^{-7}$. This assumes the density of the calcine-derived solids in the PLS approximates that of lepidolite. The POCF is $10^{-3}$ (L to m$^3$). The yield rate is 0.80, a conservative rough estimate reflecting the lower precipitation efficiency expected for a sulfate-route PLS relative to a chloride brine. Laboratory-scale literature for lepidolite PLS reports recoveries of approximately 90\% under near-optimal conditions~\cite{dengSystematicReviewSulfate2026}, with worse performance expected in a generic all-chemical impurity removal route. This parameter represents a priority area for refinement.
 
Soda ash is dosed at 16.55 kg \ce{Na2CO3} per kg Li, targeting the Li constituent. This corresponds to 3.11 t \ce{Na2CO3} per tonne \ce{Li2CO3} produced (verified: $0.1879 \times 16.55 = 3.11$ t/t), representing a $2.17\times$ stoichiometric excess. The higher excess relative to the chloride route is justified by the elevated ionic strength in sulfate PLS and is consistent with the Thacker Pass disclosure~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}; we assume it is also reasonable for a lepidolite sulfate-route PLS.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Thickening and Dewatering.}\quad

The thickening and dewatering step concentrates the \ce{Li2CO3} slurry from approximately 20 wt\% solids to approximately 70 wt\% solids via gravity settling and mechanical pressing, recovering mother liquor for recycle or discharge. The primary input is \texttt{li2co3\_slurry} at 20 wt\% solids, with input and step CCFs of $2.24 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm), derived as $w_s \cdot \rho_\text{mix} / 10^3$ at 20 wt\% solids with \ce{Li2CO3} grain density 2.11 t/m$^3$: $\rho_\text{mix} = 1/(0.2/2.11 + 0.8/1.0) = 1.118$ t/m$^3$.
 
The output \texttt{li2co3\_cake} is at 70 wt\% solids with density $\rho_\text{mix} = 1/(0.7/2.11 + 0.3/1.0) = 1.583$ t/m$^3$, giving an output CCF of $0.7 \times 1.583 / 10^3 = 1.108 \times 10^{-3}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm). The POCF is $0.202$, derived as $(0.2 \times \rho_{20\%}) / (0.7 \times \rho_{70\%}) = (0.2 \times 1.118) / (0.7 \times 1.583)$, representing the volumetric contraction from 20 wt\% to 70 wt\% solids. One reagent is consumed: flocculant at 0.005 kg/m$^3$ of slurry feed, a rough estimate.
 
The mother liquor overflow is treated differently by pathway. For the chloride (brine) route, the overflow is assumed to be recycled back to the precipitation reactor or the evaporation pond system, consistent with the Silver Peak  disclosure \cite{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a}; the conversion factor is therefore set to zero and no wastewater treatment cost is incurred. For the sulfate route, the overflow is discharged to wastewater treatment with a conversion factor of $0.80$ m$^3$/m$^3$ of slurry feed, consistent with the calculated volume fraction removed during thickening ($\sim$0.80 m$^3$/m$^3$). This water balance is a priority area for refinement \ref{sec:04-limitations}, particularly for Thacker Pass, where water recycling is a central design feature of the project-specific flowsheet~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}.

This step uses the \texttt{mechanical\_dewatering} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-dewater}).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Washing and purification.}\quad

The washing and purification step removes residual mother liquor and soluble impurities from the \ce{Li2CO3} filter cake by counter-washing with process water, improving product purity. The primary input is \texttt{li2co3\_cake} at 70 wt\% solids, with input, step, and output CCFs all equal to $1.108 \times 10^{-3}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm), consistent with the thickening output. The POCF and yield rate are 1.0; washing does not change the cake volume or lithium content materially at this stage.
 
Process water is consumed as a wash input; the dosage is a rough estimate. The wash liquor is handled by pathway: for the chloride route it is recycled back to the precipitation reactor or pond system per the Silver Peak environmental disclosure~\cite{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a}, with conversion factor zero; for the sulfate route it is discharged to wastewater treatment at $0.1$ m$^3$/m$^3$ of cake, reflecting the pass-through of wash water after contact with the cake. The non-recycling assumption for the sulfate route is a conservative simplification and represents a refinement priority alongside the rest of the water balance.

This step uses the \texttt{wash\_purify\_combo} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-washpurify}).

 
\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Drying and packaging.}\quad

The drying and packaging step removes residual moisture from the washed \ce{Li2CO3} cake and prepares the final battery-grade product. The primary input is \texttt{lithium\_carbonate\_cake} in m$^3$ at 70 wt\% solids, converted to the step basis in tonnes via an IPCF of $1.583$ t/m$^3$, equal to the cake density $\rho_\text{mix} = 1/(0.7/2.11 + 0.3/1.0)$; this represents the theoretical maximum density of a 70 wt\% \ce{Li2CO3} cake and likely slightly overestimates the actual bulk density in practice. The input CCF is $1.108 \times 10^{-3}$ kg constituent per (m$^3 \cdot$ppm), consistent with the upstream steps. The step CCF is $7.0 \times 10^{-4}$ kg constituent per (tonne$\cdot$ppm), derived as $w_s / 10^3 = 0.7 / 10^3$, reflecting the mass fraction of \ce{Li2CO3} in the cake on a tonne basis.
 
The output \texttt{lithium\_carbonate\_product} is dry \ce{Li2CO3} in tonnes, with a POCF of $0.7$ representing the \ce{Li2CO3} mass fraction of the input cake, and an output CCF of $1.0 \times 10^{-3}$ kg constituent per (tonne$\cdot$ppm), the standard conversion for a pure product on a mass basis. No reagents are consumed. Evaporated moisture is released to the atmosphere as a coproduct with negligible cost or emissions impact.

This step uses the \texttt{drying\_packaging\_generic} machine block (\ref{app:04-methods-blockdefs-drypack}).

\paragraph{Tailings and Residue Management}\label{app:04-methods-facilitychoices-tailings}

Tailings and residue management are key contributors to the cost and environmental impacts of minerals extraction. We model this step differentially across each pathway type, given spodumene, lepidolite, clay, and brine-hosted deposits each have their own typical mineralizations and byproducts \citep{khakmardanComparativeLifeCycle2023, chordiaLifeCycleEnvironmental2022, setuEnvironmentalImpactAssessment2026, royLithiumClayAssessing2024}. Tailings management practices vary across projects, ranging from conventional slurry impoundments to filtered dry-stack systems, and depend on factors such as water availability, seismic risk, climatic conditions, and regulatory constraints. Ore-based processing generates solid residues from comminution, beneficiation, and leaching operations. These residues must be dewatered, transported, and permanently stored in accordance with site-specific geotechnical and environmental requirements. Furthermore, in the production of lithium from pregnant leach solutions, additional residues are generated, which need to be managed. 

Due to the fact that modeling tailings inherently deals with the management of coproducts rather than primary outputs, in this work, we do not (currently) model this as a model block to fit within the \texttt{ProductionStep} paradigm, but rather as a custom function parameterized by residue tonnage, solids concentration, and water recovery fraction. Tailings handling, thickening, filtration, and disposal infrastructure are therefore aggregated, preserving sensitivity to ore grade, recovery efficiency, and leach residue generation while avoiding over-specification of storage facility design.

We parameterize each residue stream independently by its dry mass (derived from the process stoichiometry at each production step), a unit disposal cost $c_i$ (\$/t dry), and a solids fraction $s$ governing transport volume and handling cost. Total tailings cost for each residue stream is then
\begin{equation}
    C_{\text{tail}} = m_{\text{chem}} \cdot \Big(c_\text{dew}(s) + c_{\text{disp}} \cdot \frac{1}{s} \Big)
    \label{eq:c-tailings}
\end{equation}
\noindent where $m_{\text{chem}}$ are annual dry solid masses (t/yr), and $c_{\text{disp}}$ reflects disposal costs for the chemical residue fraction, and 1/$s$ converts the gate fee from \$/t wet to \$/t dry. The solids correction factor $s$ scales cost to the wet mass actually handled. $c_\text{dew}(s)$ is the unit dewatering costs, derived from estimated dewatering operating costs for sludge from the thickener and mechanical dewatering block models. 

For wet sludge streams ($s < 0.55$), dewatering costs range from approximately \$8---17/t DS for gravity thickening ($s \in [0.08, 0.15]$), \$17--30/t DS for belt filter press ($s \in [0.15, 0.25]$), and \$30--48/t DS for plate-and-frame filter press ($s \in [0.25, 0.50]$). For dry-stack streams ($s \geq 0.55$), stacking costs range from \$3--8/t DS for paste pumping and conveyor stacking ($s \in [0.55, 0.75]$), declining to \$1.5--4/t DS for dry conveyor-only handling ($s \in [0.75, 1.00]$). Dewatering electricity consumption is estimated within the cost model, ranging from approximately 3--8 kWh/t DS for gravity thickening to 35--60 kWh/t DS for plate-and-frame pressing, and the associated electricity cost is incorporated into the total sink handling cost via the facility-level electricity price. However, the corresponding Scope 2 greenhouse gas emissions from dewatering electricity are not currently computed, as the electricity consumption estimate is not passed to the emissions accounting framework.

Disposal gate fees are drawn from China's National Development and Reform Commission for hazardous secure landfill costs in Jiangxi province~\cite{nationaldevelopmentandreformcommission2024}. GHG emissions from waste disposal sinks are not currently modeled; this represents a limitation discussed in Section \ref{sec:limitations}.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Type 1: Evaporative Residue (Brines)}\quad

Waste streams from brine production primarily consist of salts generated through precipitation during the evaporative phase, and brine-derived chemical residues generated during concentration, chemical treatment, and selective lithium extraction. Solid volumes are from evaporation low relative to ore-based pathways, but still significant given the volume of brine that needs to be processed per tonne output, with a strong dependence on brine composition, especially the ratio of Magnesium to Lithium in the feed. 

Firstly, as brine is concentrated across a series of solar ponds, major salts precipitate sequentially in approximate order of decreasing solubility, and are harvested periodically from the pond floors \citep{flexerLithiumRecoveryBrines2018, garrettHandbookLithiumNatural2004, becerraTechnicalReportSummary2024}. Depending on the brine composition, this order is typically halite (\ce{NaCl}), gypsum (\ce{CaSO4 * 2H2O}), sylvite (\ce{KCl}), and carnallite (\ce{KCl * MgCl2 * 6H2O}). These evaporation salt precipitates are physically stable and chemically non-reactive; at operations they are typically restacked adjacent to ponds, and thus incur primarily mechanical haulage costs. We thus model these as primary maintenance costs of the evaporation ponds, and do not explicitly model them using this tailings model.

For the chemical residues, primarily slurries of \ce{Mg(OH)2} and \ce{CaCO3}, the stream is modeled at $s = 0.35$, for dewatering to a stackable, nonhazardous filter cake composed of these precipitated salts. This stream is routed to an on-site stack \cite{srkconsultingSECTechnicalReport2023a}, with an on-site containment facility operating cost adder applied.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Type 2: Leach Residue (Clay)}\quad

Clay leaching generates two primary solid waste streams: an impurity sludge from the neutralization and impurity removal steps, and a coarse leach residue from the counter-current decantation (CCD) circuit. The impurity sludge, consisting primarily of magnesium and calcium hydroxide precipitates with residual sulfate, is modeled at $s=0.25$. This stream is routed to an on-site lined containment facility at Thacker Pass rather than off-site disposal, consistent with the project disclosure~\cite{sgscanadaincSK1300Technical2024}; the containment facility operating cost is applied as a flat adder of \$5/t DS, which is a placeholder pending more detailed permit and operational data. The coarse CCD filter cake, consisting of spent claystone with low residual lithium and reagent content, is modeled at $s=0.65$, and routed to an on-site dry stack.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Type 3: Complex Reactive Ore Residue (Lepidolite)}\quad

Lepidolite processing generates three residue streams with distinct handling requirements. The impurity sludge from staged neutralization --- containing metal hydroxide precipitates including aluminum, iron, and fluoride-bearing compounds --- is classified as hazardous waste under Chinese regulations due to elevated concentrations of thallium, rubidium, and cesium~\cite{sulcaLithiumPricesWhere2024, fisherScrutinisingLithiumTechnology2022}. This stream is modeled at $s=0.20$, representative of thickener underflow prior to mechanical dewatering, and routed to an off-site hazardous secure landfill with a gate fee of \$280/t wet (\textasciitilde\$1,400/t DS at this solids fraction). This stream drives the majority of the tailings cost reported for Jianxiawo. 
The classification and flotation coarse reject (dilute gangue slurry at approximately $s=0.30$) is routed to an on-site conventional wet tailings storage facility (TSF), which does not require lining under Chinese standards for non-hazardous lepidolite gangue; operating costs are estimated at \$4/t DS for earthwork, monitoring, and water reclaim, plus 2 kWh/t DS for slurry pumping. These numbers are also placeholder estimates pending more detailed permit and operational data, especially given ongoing regulatory tightening around Lepidolite permitting~\cite{benchmarkteamCATLSuspendsOperations, zhangChinaHasDomestic2025}. Finally, the leach residue from the CCD circuit is modeled at $s=0.65$ and routed to on-site dry stacking, consistent with the coarse clay residue handling described above.

\clearpage
\subsubsection{Machine Model Block Methodology}\label{app:04-methods-machineblocks}

\paragraph{Model Block Data Aggregation Methodology}\label{app:04-methods-machineblocks-aggregation}

This study employs a structured bottom-up aggregation methodology to translate heterogeneous sources of industry data (technical reports, specification sheets, regulatory filings, and interviews where appropriate) into internally consistent technoeconomic model blocks, with each \texttt{ProductionStep} object having various model block options. The objective is not necessarily to replicate any singular plant configuration, but to construct reasonable baselines for representative, transparent, and sensitivity-aware unit operations suitable for high-level comparative analysis. Using scaling factors or parallel production lines, these  blocks can then be sized to meet desired demand.

For each model block, two distinct but co-defined components are specified:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Machine block options] Determines equipment sizing and associated capital and operational requirements.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Input and output materials] Specifies the mass streams entering  and leaving the block.
\end{description}

These components are interconnected but decomposable: in the absence of detailed engineering...

Four major steps go into the creation of each block: (1) definition of bill of equipment and key inputs and outputs; (2) raw data collection; (3) aggregation into model blocks, including scenario development (low/mid/high);  and (4) validation and cross-checking.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 1: Define a Bill of Equipment and Key Inputs and Outputs.}\quad

The machine block is composed of a Bill of Equipment (BOE), which identifies:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Primary process equipment] Singular or a key set of core process machines.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Required secondary systems] e.g., handling, dosing, recirculation, discharge.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Balance-of-plant auxiliary elements] e.g., piping, controls, structural and access.
\end{description}

There are three types of inputs:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Primary inputs] (e.g., ore, brine, slurry) defined with a calculated flow rate or batch size and associated cycle time.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Secondary inputs] i.e., reagents (e.g., lime, soda ash, sulfuric acid), defined with calculated dosages based on stoichiometric analyses with operational excess factors, inefficiencies, and possible recycle simplifications.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Utilities] (e.g., process water, compressed air, natural gas) calculated as inputs that are fully consumed unless otherwise specified as a co-product.
\end{description}

There are likewise two types of outputs:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Primary outputs] (e.g., excavated ore, pumped brine, dewatered slurry) the assumed result of the reaction of primary inputs, secondary inputs, and additional machine-specific processing.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Secondary outputs] i.e., co-products (e.g., \ce{CO2} emissions, wastewater, tailings) calculated based on stoichiometric analyses.
\end{description}

As discussed in the production step formulation, primary and secondary inputs and outputs can have constituents defined. While calculation of reagent usage and co-product production are carried out in Step 3, the definition of key inputs and outputs is performed here, as they affect the engineering design and assumptions made in the model.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 2: Raw Data Collection.}\quad

Raw data are compiled from:

\begin{description}[noitemsep, topsep=0pt]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Feasibility studies and technical reports] SEC Technical Report Summaries (SK-1300) and NI 43-101 reports.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Vendor specification sheets and package data]
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Standard engineering references] e.g., the Australasian Institute of Mining and Metallurgy's \textit{Cost Estimation Handbook}; \citet{petersPlantDesignEconomics1991}.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Literature sources] where detailed data exist.
    \item[\textbullet\enskip Interviews and technical discussions] with operators, vendors, and other modelers.
\end{description}

The primary sources for each modeling block are listed at the top of each modeling block.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 3: Aggregate into Model Blocks.}\quad

Raw data are aggregated into standardized model blocks using a consistent structure:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Block output} = f(\text{equation of merit},\ \text{volume defining basis})
\end{equation}
Each model block provides a baseline for: the volume defining basis (i.e., the design variable, such as slurry flow rate, solids throughput, working volume, or other key sizing metric); core physical relationships as a function of the volume defining basis (e.g., mixing intensity, stoichiometric demand); installed capacity with oversizing margin; and CAPEX and OPEX components.

Given the heterogeneous and uncertain nature of raw data, we develop an envelope approach to estimate conservative (pessimistic), midpoint, and optimistic values for key parameters, anchored to reported values with uncertainty bounds introduced transparently. These envelopes help capture extremes of performance and encompass the possible space of models. Assumptions are stated explicitly to maintain traceability.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{CAPEX Aggregation.}\quad

While capital equipment costs are not a primary driver of this analysis given the focus on operational competitiveness, they are nonetheless important as a point of comparison against established studies, and operational maintenance costs are often sized as a percentage of capital costs. We use an anchor equipment approach, consistent with standard chemical and mineral processing practice:
\begin{equation}
    C_{\text{total}} = C_{\text{anchor}} + C_{\text{auxiliary}}
\end{equation}
where $C_{\text{anchor}}$ is primary equipment cost derived from vendor data, scaling relationships, and/or reported feasibility values, and $C_{\text{auxiliary}}$ represents explicit adders for integration and balance-of-plant elements.

Auxiliary CAPEX adders typically cover systems such as pumps and piping, structural steel and platforms, instrumentation and controls, electrical distribution, and reagent handling and dosing. Adders are applied as either explicit percentage ranges derived from standard cost breakdowns, or discrete subsystem packages when vendor costs are available. Standard default estimates are given in \ref{tab:c-capex-adders} drawn from Table 4 and Table 6 of Chapter 6 of \citet{petersPlantDesignEconomics1991}. Where possible, equipment-specific adders from \citet{petersPlantDesignEconomics1991} were used. 

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Standard auxiliary CAPEX adder ranges by equipment class and scenario.}
\label{tab:c-capex-adders}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\hline
\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{Mechanically simple,} & \textbf{Mechanically intensive,} & \textbf{Thermal systems,} \\
                  & \textbf{pump-dominated}       & \textbf{multiple-drive systems}  & \textbf{fans/ducts/controls} \\
\hline
Conservative & 50\% & 60\% & 55\% \\
Midpoint     & 35\% & 45\% & 40\% \\
Optimistic   & 20\% & 30\% & 25\% \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Maintenance Estimation.}\quad

Maintenance is estimated as an annual cost, as a percentage of all-in fixed-capital investment costs, depending on the complexity of operation. We directly quote maintenance costs for chemical processes from \citet{petersPlantDesignEconomics1991}, but as not all of the processes modeled in this system are necessarily chemical in nature, we construct a similar table for physical processes (e.g., bulk ore crushing and processing, material movement, pumping and other auxiliaries), based on the SME Mining Engineering Handbook \citep{darlingSMEMiningEngineering2011}. 

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Maintenance cost as \% of fixed-capital investment (annual basis)}
\label{tab:c-maintenace-fraction}
\begin{tabular}{p{7cm}ccc}
\hline
\textbf{Type of operation} & \textbf{Wages} & \textbf{Materials} & \textbf{Total} \\
\hline
Simple chemical process & 1--3 & 1--3 & 2--6 \\
Average chemical processes with normal operating conditions & 2--4 & 3--5 & 5--9 \\
Complicated chemical processes, severe corrosion, or extensive instrumentation & 3--5 & 4--6 & 7--11 \\
Materials movement only & 0.5--1 & 0 & 0.5--1 \\
Simple mechanical processes & 4 & 1--3 & 5--7 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Labor Requirements Estimation.}\quad

Operating labor requirements are estimated using published operator-per-shift factors
and a staffing multiplier; the full method is described in
\ref{app:04-methods-machineblocks-labor}.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Electricity Estimation.}\quad

Electrical demand for each block is decomposed analogously to CAPEX:
\begin{equation}
    P_{\text{total}} = P_{\text{anchor}} + P_{\text{auxiliary}}
\end{equation}
The maximum electrical demand for each machine is typically reported in specification sheets; load factors (LF) are estimated to approximate average power consumption during operation. Auxiliary electrical loads are estimated using an adder structure analogous to CAPEX, but with different ranges reflecting the distinct physical meaning of electrical auxiliaries. Standard default estimates are given in \ref{tab:c-elec-adders}.

\begin{table}[h]
\caption{Standard auxiliary electrical load adder ranges by equipment class and scenario.}
\label{tab:c-elec-adders}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\hline
\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{Mechanically simple,} & \textbf{Mechanically intensive,} & \textbf{Thermal systems,} \\
                  & \textbf{pump-dominated}       & \textbf{multiple-drive systems}  & \textbf{fans/ducts/controls} \\
\hline
Conservative & 25\% & 55\% & 65\% \\
Midpoint     & 15\% & 40\% & 50\% \\
Optimistic   & 10\% & 25\% & 35\% \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Other Utilities.}\quad

The use of other utilities (water, compressed air, natural gas, steam, etc.) varies significantly across blocks depending on the process and equipment being modeled. Utility usage is derived from project disclosures, mass balance or thermodynamic calculations, and vendor specifications. Where appropriate, the same anchor-plus-adder approach is applied: identifying the dominant utility consumer, anchoring to reported usage, and applying auxiliary adders as needed.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 4: Validation and Cross-Checking.}\quad

Model outputs are validated through cross-comparison with published feasibility studies, academic literature, and grey literature; internal consistency checks across unit operations; sensitivity testing of dominant parameters. This iterative validation ensures that no block is isolated from industrial reality, while preserving transparency about sources of data and uncertainty. While interviews and discussions with industry professionals and other modelers informed initial design choices and some machine block designs, additional discussions could further validate the model inputs and results.
